=================== ROUND 8: THE FLASH-ON-24GB FEASIBILITY VERDICT + THE MEASUREMENT CORRECTION (6c7ef99) ==================
FLASH FEASIBILITY (the checkpoint IS Flash: Glm5Next, 175.6GB packed — routed experts 156.3GB/89%,
attn+dense 13.5GB, embed 2.5GB, shared 2.2GB; per-expert packed 12.6MB; per decode step one token
touches 8x42x12.6MB = 4.2GB packed):
- FULL RESIDENCY: NO. 156GB of packed experts vs 24GB — even a quality-destroying 2bpw re-quant
  (39GB) does not fit. The "no-go" is correct for resident-weights designs.
- THE STREAMING DESIGN ALREADY FITS: 3-12GB GPU measured (DEVMEM over 2882 samples). Speed is the
  constraint, not the card: each expert visit deqs 12.6MB packed -> ~100MB f32 materialized (8-10x
  traffic multiplier).
- THE FEASIBLE PLAY: YES, CONDITIONALLY. Resident attn/dense/embed (~16GB bf16, or ~10GB int8
  attention) + experts streamed PACKED from the host page cache (125GB RAM; 4.2GB/step touched)
  into a FUSED DEQUANT-GEMM (dequant in registers, no f32 materialization): expert traffic/step
  ~70GB -> ~8.5GB -> decode step ~10-15s -> endpoint ~1-1.5min. THE LINCHPIN IS THE KERNEL: a
  fused dequant-gemm over the .mcg/EXL3 trellis format. CRITICAL SUBSIDY: the exl3dq1s phase-2
  4-rows-per-thread variant measured a 28x DEQ speedup and was reverted with a KNOWN bug signature
  (max_abs exactly 8388608 = kn; the comment in exl3dq.vyb) — fixing it is the highest-value
  known optimization INDEPENDENT of the fusion, and the fusion builds on it.
- HOST-SIDE NOTE: the checkpoint (175.6GB) exceeds the 125GB RAM — the page cache cannot hold it
  all; the hot expert set per request (~dozens of GB) fits, cold experts hit disk. NFS/disk speed
  becomes the tail latency term for cold experts.
MEASUREMENT CORRECTION (honesty): the SEC instrumentation spans vary ~9s run-to-run on IDENTICAL
numeric state (best2's DIGESTENTRY digest exactly equals increment-2's 2944b0612c, yet its SEC sum
differed +8.7s from the same code measured hours earlier). The per-increment "-11s cumulative"
claims of rounds 7b-7f are RETRACTED as unmeasurable. VERIFIED ONLY: the phase-1 expert f32 win in
the T_MOE same-position timers (deq 36.7->34.8s, mm 3.5->3.3s, consistent across many samples)
and the round-3 deq-fusion win (-40s, measured back-to-back in a quiet era). The f32 arc's
attention/dense/decode-side increments were reverted to f64 (their effects sit inside the noise
band); the landed state = expert-f32 in tail_site only + the fast resolve + the dense step-2
cache. Gates green: A 3.665e-6, B 1.204e-4, draft 576 = oracle.
THE DECISION FRAME RICK SET: feasibility proven conditionally -> the go/no-go gate is the fused-
dequant kernel microbench (one expert shape, 12.6MB packed -> nt x 4096, vs the 80us gemm32
reference). If the microbench passes -> implement (resident attn + the fused kernel per shape +
serve rewiring, multi-session). If not -> the Spark cluster continues + an online provider for
the testing duration.
=================== ROUND 9: THE FLASH FEASIBILITY GATE — ANSWERED (7838748) ==================
The feasibility gate Rick set (the fused-dequant microbench) is ANSWERED: NEGATIVE for the
projected speedup. Sequence: (1) the 4-row phase-2 deq variant was FIXED (the coverage bug from
88ff324 understood — each CTA owns its rows in BOTH phases; the reverted version read rows its
phase 1 never wrote): exl3dq1s4, grid=ntiles/4, 8 rows/CTA, 4 dequants + 4 outputs per thread,
H loaded once per c2. The probe: BIT-IDENTICAL on 8.4M elements (0 diffs); the in-serve run
reproduces the exact pre-change digests (2944b0612c), gates green. (2) The speedup: -0.4s of
34.7s (-1%), NOT 28x. The deq is dominated by phase-1's scattered 4-nibble gathers (~2ns/element,
latency-bound) — the 36.3s->1.3s claim in 88ff324's message was wrong or measured something else.
(3) CONSEQUENCE: the fused-dequant-gemm projection is INVALIDATED — its win was eliminating the
f32 materialization traffic, but the deq is COMPUTE-bound, so fusion buys ~the mm time (~3.4s),
not 10x. THE FLASH-ON-24GB VERDICT: NOT FEASIBLE at the projected speed on the current
architecture. The endpoint floor with the current deq design is ~3min. The only remaining path
to usable speed is a from-scratch GATHER-RESTRUCTURED deq kernel: re-pack the checkpoint offline
so a warp's elements come from contiguous trellis bytes (coalesced gathers), then a matching
kernel — a genuine R&D project with uncertain outcome, cleanly decoupled from the serve (the
re-pack is offline; the kernel slots into exl3_deq32f's dq1s launch).
PER RICK'S DECISION FRAME: continue development on the Spark cluster; switch the model to an
online provider for the testing duration. The 24GB streaming serve remains correct-and-green
(draft 576, gates green) at ~3-5min/request for offline/batch use.

TRANSPORT (native/host/transport_probe.vyb): Vyb-native TCP over the CX7 fabric, lefty
192.168.192.1:9143 <-> righty 192.168.192.2. Loopback 7.5 GB/s / 15 us RTT. FABRIC: burst
~9.1 GB/s (9142 MB/s) but the transfer TRUNCATES at the 16 MiB socket-buffer boundary — the
stdlib's socket_send returns the BUFFERED count and silently drops the remainder (server drains
16,777,280 B then EOF; the client's ack-wait gets "" and RTT fails). FIX BEFORE P6 ALL-GATHER:
socket_send must loop on partial writes (or the caller must chunk by SO_SNDBUF), and socket_close
needs a linger policy so a close racing unsent data cannot RST the stream. This is a STDLIB bug
worth fixing upstream (Vyb), not a fabric limit — the burst shows the link is fine.
=== ROUND 10 ADDENDUM: P6 TRANSPORT GREEN ON THE FABRIC (correction + numbers) ===
CORRECTION TO ROUND 10: the "16 MiB truncation" was the PROBE's arithmetic (the client sent
mb x 64 KiB while the server waited for mb MiB), not a fabric limit. The time was not wasted: it
exposed a REAL runtime bug — __vyb_net_send (the String socket path) did a single send() and
returned the short count, where the Vec/bytes path always looped. FIXED in
runtime/vyb_runtime.c (loop over short writes + EINTR, MSG_NOSIGNAL define hoisted) and pushed on
Vyb branch fix/net-send-short-writes (54f3d0c0; main is ruleset-protected — PR to land).
PROVEN WITH THE BYTES API (socket_send_bytes has always looped; view(buf) borrow, no copies):
  loopback godzilla: 256 MiB in 32 ms = 8.0 GB/s, ack 2 B, RTT 200 rounds avg 10 us.
  FABRIC lefty 192.168.192.1 -> righty 192.168.192.2 (one CX7 rail, plain TCP): 256 MiB in
  53 ms = 4.8 GB/s (~38 Gbps), RX matches at 54 ms, ack OK, RTT 50 us, 200/200 rounds.
=> THE DISTRIBUTED-INFERENCE TRANSPORT IS GREEN. Next P6 work: (a) rebuild both Sparks' compilers
so they carry the runtime fix (the bytes API works without it; the String path needs it);
(b) the all-gather itself (per-layer activation exchange: tiny payloads — H x R x f64 = 229 KB per
layer per rank, so 45 x 2 x ~50us RTT + 0.5 ms transfer per step — latency-dominated, so the
transport's job is done once the RTT is 50 us); (c) split the model (layer/TP or expert-parallel)
and gate TP2_VYB_MATCH against the P4 single-layer output.

=== ROUND 12: P6 INCREMENT 1 — THE RANK PROBE (f58ffd7) ==================
native/host/p6_rank_probe.vyb: a self-contained expert-partition driver. gx_resolve via the
index.json (131 ms, NO shard walk — far better than the serve's tab build), exl3_deq32f +
mm_host32 for one expert's FFN at layer 3 with a deterministic x, digest exchange over the
proven transport, and the gate is rank 0 recomputing the PEER expert locally (rel <= 1e-12).
Engine gains two exported helpers so drivers need ZERO direct FFI: launch_kernel(f,g,pb) and
mapdev_build(finvk, mapd) (the serve's inline map setup, exported verbatim).

Minimal repro (imports tensor + a local `extern "C" { cuMemAlloc_v2 }`, one freedom alloc) RUNS
ON BOTH NODES ("MIN_OK") — so libcuda resolution and plain freedom FFI are fine on lefty.
The failing unit is specifically glm_engine's compile unit on lefty's build2 binary:

=== ROUND 14: P6 INCREMENT 2 GREEN — THE ROUTED MoE MIX, BIT-EXACT ACROSS THE SPARKS (Vyb 34eae310 / VybForge next) ===
8 routed experts at layer 3 split 4/4 across lefty (rank 0) and righty (rank 1) with fixed
router weights [.31 .24 .19 .14 .06 .03 .02 .01] + the bf16 shared expert. Each rank folds its
own 4 weighted expert FFNs into a 4096 f64 partial; rank 1 ships its partial as %.17g
length-prefixed text; rank 0 adds it to its own and gates against a local recompute of BOTH
halves in the same left-fold order: ndiff=0 max_abs=0 of 4096 — BIT-EXACT. MIX digest
-20.218431346918848 (full precision). Partial fold on each side ~1.4 s warm; rank-0 reference +
shared expert add ~37 s (5 more expert deqs + the bf16 shared).
COMPILER (Vyb 34eae310): .to_string_precise() formats %.17g (round-trip exact; %g lost ~6 digits
on every text wire) and .to_float() now binds the C library strtod — the stdlib aspect's
hand-rolled digit accumulator was 1 ULP off on 25% of f64 inputs (979/4096 measured) which is
what the first exchange exposed as ndiff=1048 max_abs=1.7e-18. Fast path intercepts in cgen
before generic aspect-method resolution, String receivers only. Verified 4096/4096 round-trip.
LESSON (bit-exactness is a three-layer property): format, parse, AND compute must all be exact.
The compute layer was already bit-identical across the two GB10s; the wire was not.
NEXT: increment 3 — the full layer (attention + this MoE) with rank 1 running the tail, then
TP2_VYB_MATCH against the single-node P4 layer numerics; experts per rank scale to 144 each.

=== COMPETITIVE BAR (Rick, standing): vLLM runs this same model at ~45-50 tps on the TensorFold rig ===
The Vyb/TensorFold path MUST BEAT ~50 tok/s decode on the same model or the port has no purpose.
That is 20-22 ms/token end to end, i.e. the whole 45-layer forward at 20 ms — every layer
(attention + MoE dequant + routed experts) must average under ~440 us. For scale: the current
single-node Vyb serve measures its decode step in SECONDS (35 s deq + gemm at the last profile),
so the gap is ~100x and the work is architectural (streaming/fusion/parallelism), not tuning.
Track every future timing against this number and state it plainly: ms/token vs the 20 ms bar.
=== ROUND 15: P6 INCREMENT 3 GREEN — REAL ROUTER, LANE-SPLIT MoE TAIL, BIT-EXACT ===
The engine's own routing rules (moe_tail_step): logits = x @ gate.weight^T (288 experts, bf16),
sigma, selection score = sigma + e_score_correction_bias, greedy top-8, weight = sigma/sum x 2.5.
Both nodes compute the router (cheap, ~0.3 s) and agree exactly: picks 153 130 115 182 | 156 184
14 195. THE SPLIT IS BY RANKING LANE (0..3 rank 0, 4..7 rank 1), not by expert id range — the picks
are spread over all 288, so any static range split would be load-imbalanced and shard-hostile.
Each rank deqs its 4 lane experts, folds weighted, ships 88,436 B exact text; rank 0 adds, adds the
bf16 shared expert, gates vs local recompute of all 8 lanes: ndiff=0 max_abs=0 BIT-EXACT. MIX digest
-1.0366555427207755. NOTE the 179,535 ms total in that run is WALL TIME INCLUDING a manual ~3 min
gap before rank 1 was launched — NOT a benchmark number. Real timing needs a synchronized launch.
STORAGE: rank 1 now holds shards 3+4+5 (layer 3's gate/bias/shared + the lane experts live across
them). Righty 385 GB free.
NEXT (throughput, against the 45-50 tok/s vLLM bar): synchronized-launch timing of this split; then
fold the split into moe_tail_step itself (env-gated expert-lane range + the exchange at the end of
the tail) so the SERVE runs it; then attention across nodes.
=== ROUND 16: SYNCHRONIZED TWO-NODE TIMING (first honest numbers vs the 45-50 tok/s bar) ===
Layer-3 MoE tail, 8 routed experts + bf16 shared, real router, synchronized launch:
  solo one node   : 7,036 ms (6,317 experts + 719 shared)
  two-node rank 0 : 3,453 ms own 4 experts; +755 ms shared (serial) = 4,208; peer 88 KB at 6,147
  gate reference  : +2,971 ms (4 more experts, gate-only, not part of a served step)
Expert compute scales 1.8x (6.3 -> 3.5 s). WALL is 6.1 s only because rank 1 JIT-compiles at
launch (~2 s, once per process, not per step) -> steady-state ~4.3 s vs 7.0 s solo (1.6x).
THE BAR: 45-50 tok/s = 20-22 ms/token for the ENTIRE 45-layer forward. One layer's MoE tail at
4.3 s is ~4,000x off. The fabric is NOT the problem (88 KB in ms). ~860 ms/expert is: the deq+gemm
chain by itself. Reaching the bar needs ~50 us/expert => dequant fused into tensor-core MMA
(Blackwell sm_121: FP4/FP6/FP8 + tcgen05) with all routed experts batched into ONE launch.
The current per-expert serial deq->gemm path is a corrrectness harness, not a performance base.
Also: solo and split digests differ at 1e-13 relative (-1.0366555427207942 vs -1.0366555427207755)
because they fold in different association order ((0..7) vs (0..3)+(4..7)); the split's own gate
(ndiff=0 vs a recompute in matching order) is the correctness proof, not digest equality.
NEXT: (a) move the shared expert off rank 0 (idle-rank work), (b) batch the 8 expert FFNs into one
launch per rank, (c) measure deq-equivalent bytes/s to size the fused-MMA target.
=== ROUND 17: THE 700 ms/EXPERT MYSTERY — gx_resolve, not the GPU (37x) ===
Instrumented the r3 probe per phase. Per expert: deq 13-14 ms, gemm 1-2 ms, tensor read 4-5 ms
-- but the loop advanced ~800 ms/expert. The cost was gx_resolve(dir,...): it re-opens and
re-parses a 2 GB shard header for EVERY call (710-798 ms/name). Fix: use the production TAB
(gx_resolve_tab + the prebuilt per-layer table in native/out/gx_tabs.cache, built by the serve;
also copied to both Sparks; righty needs its own copy because its checkpoint dir differs, and
the tab is path-independent). Result: resolve 700 -> 1 ms; SOLO 8 experts + shared = 7044 -> 188 ms
(37x), digest IDENTICAL. Two-node: rank0 half 81 ms (+32 shared), rank1 85+34 ms, wall 2.1 s only
because rank 1 JIT-compiles at launch (~2 s, once per process). Steady-state step ~125 ms = 1.5x
vs solo 188 ms. Gate EXACT.
LESSON: measure the phases before optimising the kernel. Per-expert resolve through gx_resolve is a
2 GB-header parse; only gx_resolve_tab / the window path are viable in any hot loop.
REMAINING: 13 ms/expert deq (12.6 MB => ~0.9 GB/s effective vs ~270 GB/s HBM) is now the dominant
term: 8 experts x 45 layers = 4.7 s/token. The bar (20-22 ms/token) needs ~50 us/expert, i.e. the
dequant fused into tensor-core MMA (Blackwell FP4/FP8 + tcgen05), all routed experts in one launch.
NEXT STEPS: (a) profile the deq kernel itself (ALWAYS from a clean GPU), (b) prototype a fused
deq+MMA kernel on one expert and compare against 13+2 ms, (c) synchronous-launch timing harness so
benchmarks never include JIT startup.
=== ROUND 18: DEQ KERNEL PROFILE (clean GPU, lefty) — where 13 ms/expert goes ===
Per deq call (gate_proj, 4.2 MB packed -> 8.4M f32 = 33.5 MB out, exl3dq1s4 grid 8192,
exl3had232 grid 32768):
  htod trellis+suh+svh : 0 ms (async, overlapped)
  exl3dq1s4 + sync     : 1 ms
  exl3had232 + sync    : 2 ms   <- the largest single stage (the Hadamard epilogue)
  alloc + param htod   : ~1 ms  (cuMemAlloc of the 33.5 MB f32 buffer PER CALL + 14 tiny htod)
  full call            : 4 ms  -> x3 (gate/up/down) = 12-13 ms/expert, matching the loop timers
PER EXPERT: deq 13 + gemm 1-2 + tensor read 4-5 = ~20 ms; 8 experts + shared = 188 ms (solo).
TWO CHEAP WINS: (a) the per-call 33.5 MB alloc + param htod is ~15% of the deq (cache the f32
buffers and the 6+8 param words once per expert shape); (b) fold had232 into the dq kernel's
epilogue (it already has the tile in registers) — it is a pure elementwise Hadamard on the same
output the dq just wrote, so it is currently a full 33.5 MB extra read+write round trip.
THE BAR: 50 us/expert needs ~80x on this 4 ms. SIMT gather-deq cannot get there; the fused
deq+MMA path (Blackwell FP4/FP8 tensor cores, tcgen05, all routed experts in one launch) is the
only route. Estimate: TC matmul at these shapes is ~10-30 us; the dequant must be an epilogue,
not a separate pass.
=== ROUND 19: THE TWO CHEAP WINS — one real (16%), one marginal (10%) ===
(a) BUFFER CACHING — REAL. exl3_deq32f_into / _into_h take a caller-owned f32
output buffer instead of cuMemAlloc-ing 33.5 MB per deq. deq 13 -> 10 ms/expert,
solo 8 experts + shared 188 -> 158 ms, digest BIT-IDENTICAL. NOTE the three deqs
(gate/up/down) run BEFORE their three gemms consume them, so they need THREE
distinct buffers — one shared buffer silently clobbers (caught before it shipped).
The serve's dense cache must keep the allocating path (it holds deq'd weights
across steps); _into is opt-in for sequential deq->consume loops.
(b) STAGED-SHARED HAD — MARGINAL. exl3had232h (exl3h.vyb, own module because
--kernel-shared is capped at 49152 and per-module; the 128x128 f32 tile is 64 KB
so it is split over TWO CTAs at 32 KB each): deq 10 -> 9 ms/expert. The plain
had's 2 ms is NOT L2 re-read bound — it is the 1.07 GFLOP of f32 SIMT dot at
~0.5 TFLOPS, i.e. the math itself. Kept as a bit-identical option (P6_HADH=1).
BUG WORTH REMEMBERING: the first version used blk_x()*dim_x()+tid_x() as the CTA
id (that is the THREAD id) — every CTA addressed block 256x too large, digest
-15.14 instead of -1.04. In a 1-D grid the CTA index is just blk_x().
STATE: solo MoE tail = 158 ms (8 experts + shared); per expert ~19 ms (deq 10,
gemm 1, read 4-5, resolve ~1). Two-node steady state ~1.2-1.5x faster on the split.
CONCLUSION: exactness-preserving micro-optimisation of this SIMT path is done —
the remaining 158 ms cannot reach the 20-22 ms/token bar. The next real move is a
tensor-core fused deq+MMA path, which CHANGES NUMERICS (f16/tf32 operands) and so
needs its own tolerance gate (not bit-exactness) plus a gold re-baseline.
=== ROUND 20: FUSED DECODE+MATVEC PROTOTYPE — BUILT, FAST, NOT YET CORRECT ===
THE ALGEBRA (the reason to bother): EXL3's blockwise composition is W = H_left @ T @ H_right
(H = the 128x128 Hadamard; su per row, sv per column). For decode (M=1) W must never exist:
  y = W x = H_left @ (T @ (H_right x))
so the Hadamard cost collapses from 1.07 GFLOP per projection (applied to the 2048x4096 matrix)
to 262K MACs (applied to two 128-blocks of a VECTOR), and the 33.5 MB W materialisation (plus its
33.5 MB read back) disappears. Per projection only the 4.2 MB packed read remains.
SHIPPED IN THIS ROUND: native/kernels/exl3mv.vyb (exl3xhat = sv.(H x); exl3mv1s4 = decode +
row-dot against xp with a shared reduction + atomic accumulate; exl3yhat = H yraw) and
native/host/p6r5_fusedmv.vyb (the gated prototype: old path deq-W -> mm vs new path, one
expert's gate_proj). All three kernels launch rc=0 and the mv kernel runs in 2 ms vs 4-5 ms for
the old deq+mm on the SAME projection.
STATUS: NUMERICS WRONG (yraw garbage, non-deterministic run to run; yhat output all zero).
The launch path is confirmed good (rc=0 everywhere); the suspect is the kernel-shared ADDRESS
CONVENTION: the emitted PTX shows ld.shared.f32 with a -1024 byte fold that my st_shared_f32
calls do NOT carry, so phase 2 reads a shifted slab. The existing exl3dq1s4 works because its
store and load offsets carry the same convention; my extra 4 KB reduction region and the
compact re-indexing broke that alignment. Harness time also shows FIRST-TOUCH cost: staging the
4.2 MB trellis on a freshly allocated buffer costs ~14 ms (unified-memory page faults) — a real
pipeline must warm/relocate that (in the deq path it is amortised away by reuse).
NEXT: (1) a minimal two-kernel shared-memory probe (store a sentinel at offset X, read it back)
to pin the convention, then fix exl3mv1s4's slab indexing; (2) once the gate is <=1e-5, measure
the real per-projection total; (3) only then scale to the 8-expert expert FFN.
=== ROUND 21: SHARED-CONVENTION PROBE RESULTS + FUSED-MV STILL WRONG ===
FINDING 1 (compiler, real): the working exl3dq.ptx uses store [%rd174] and load [%rd240+-1024]
with different base registers — the -1024 is BASE SELECTION, not a bias. Store/load offsets do
agree in a correct kernel. The earlier suspicion was wrong.
FINDING 2 (compiler, real): CONSTANT shared offsets get scalarised into separate variables
(__vyb_kernel_shared_$_0/_1/_3, and ld returns garbage) — so a literal-offset shared access does
NOT alias the computed-offset slab. exl3mv now uses only computed offsets and reuses the (dead)
4 KB tile slab for the reduction, so no second shared region exists at all. It STILL returns
garbage, so the shared region was not the (only) fault.
STATE OF THE FUSED MV: launches rc=0; mv kernel 2 ms vs 4-5 ms for deq+mm on gate_proj; yraw is
~1e8 (wrong scale, non-deterministic) while xp is ~1e-14 for this input — those two are mutually
inconsistent, so the defect is in the mv kernel's DECODE half (the trellis/map/inverse pointers or
the compact slab re-index), not in the vector passes.
NEXT (precise): gate the decode half ALONE — have exl3mv1s4 also write its slab tile to global for
CTA 0 and diff it element-for-element against the same tile from the known-good exl3dq1s4 (which
materialises W). That isolates decode vs matvec in one run instead of three compound guesses.
=== ROUND 22: THE GATE WAS LYING — f32 buffers read as f64 ===
bulk_dtoh reads f64; every fused-path buffer (xp, yraw, y) is f32, so every "value ~ 1e-28 / 0"
diagnostic in rounds 20-21 was a FORMAT ARTIFACT, not a numeric failure. yhat now writes f64
(st_f64) so the comparison is real.
CURRENT REAL STATE (expert 153, gate_proj, x = the deterministic ramp):
  OLD (materialise W + mm):  y0 = -0.40728, deq 5 ms
  NEW (fused xp/T/yhat):     y0 = -0.02777, mv 2 ms, total 17 ms (stage 15 = first-touch)
  gate: max_abs 1.45 vs old max 1.28 -> same scale, wrong values.
So the pipeline runs end to end at the right magnitude; one of the three transforms is
mis-assigned. Prime suspect confirmed this round: the EXL3 buffer is W^T (rows = input dim, su
per input, sv per output), so su must fold into xp and sv into yraw — the kernel now does that,
and phase 2 dots DOWN slab columns. The residual (ynew ~ 7-20x small, wrong sign pattern) points
at a transform applied on the wrong axis or a second/dropped Hadamard.
NEXT (cheap, decisive): x = one-hot e_0. Then y must equal column 0 of W, which the OLD path has
materialised — an element-wise compare with no summation cancellations to hide behind, and each
of {su into xp, sv into yraw, H axes, W^T layout} becomes individually checkable.
KERNEL FACTS WORTH KEEPING: constant shared offsets get scalarised (never use them for a slab);
the PTX -1024 fold is base selection, not a bias; the tile slab can be REUSED for a reduction only
after a barrier that follows the last tile read.
=== ROUND 23: ONE-HOT GATE — THE LAYOUT ASSUMPTION IS WRONG, AND THE OLD PATH SAYS SO ===
With x = e_0 the arithmetic must collapse to y[i] = W[i][0] = B[i][0] for ANY reasonable B layout
(y[n] = sum_k A[k] B[..] with A = e_0 keeps exactly one term). Measured:
    old_y0  =  0.00972538
    Wcol0_0 = -0.01945076   (first f32 of the deq buffer)
These DISAGREE, so neither the "B = W (out x in)" nor the "B = W^T (in x out)" model explains the
buffer: the one-hot value is not B[0][0] under either. Something in the low-level contract
(gemm32's B interpretation, the cast32 A-padding, or the deq's store order) is still unmodelled —
this is now a LAYOUT question, not a transform question.
STATE: fused path y0 = -0.00294 vs old 0.00973 (one-hot) — same scale, both wrong vs the raw
buffer. mv kernel 2 ms; stage 15 ms on first touch.
NEXT PROBE (one run, definitive): dump B[0], B[1], B[2048], B[4096] and y0, y1 with x = e_0 and
x = e_1. Whichever buffer index equals y0 identifies the true element order; whichever equals the
difference identifies the stride. Do that BEFORE touching any transform code again — two rounds
have now been spent assuming a layout.
LESSON: for a re-associated kernel the FIRST gate must be an index-to-index identity (one-hot),
not an aggregate. Every aggregate metric hid a layout error behind cancellation.
=== ROUND 24: LAYOUT PINNED — B IS W^T ([input 4096][output 2048], stride 2048) ===
Index-identity probe (x = e_i via the mm path, buffer elements dumped directly):
    y(e0)_0 = -0.01945076 == B[0]        y(e0)_1 = 0.01580474 == B[1]
    y(e1)_0 =  0.01615867 == B[2048]     (B[2048] = the next INPUT's output 0)
So B[k][n] with k = input (stride 2048), n = output. The old path is correct and consistent.
THE ROUND-23 "CONTRADICTION" WAS MY BUG: the one-shot x built x[0] from the ramp (-0.5) instead of
1.0, and 0.00973 = -0.5 x -0.01945 exactly. Lesson recorded: check the test vector itself.
FUSED STATE with the confirmed layout: the structure (xp = su.(H_in x); u = down-column dots over
the tile; y = H_out u per 128 output block; sv on u) is the algebraically correct chain. Measured
against the same one-hot: fused y0 = -0.00294 vs true 0.00973 (ratio -0.302) — still wrong, so an
implementation detail remains (candidates: an H applied on the wrong axis in xhat/yhat, a scale
applied twice, or the slab row/col mapping in phase 2).
NEXT (one run): with x = e_0 and a single input tile, dump (a) xp[0..3] and (b) the 8x128 slab
values for CTA 0, and compare xp against a host-computed H_in x with su — the vector pass is
checkable exactly, which splits "vector passes wrong" from "tile/decode wrong" for good.
=== ROUND 25: THE VECTOR PASSES ARE CORRECT — the defect is in the tile decode or yhat ===
x = e_0, dumped device-side and compared against the closed form xp[i] = su[i].H[i][0]:
    i=0  H=0.08838834  su=0.01782227  xp=0.0015752805629  expected=0.0015752805829
    i=1  H=0.08838834  su=0.01770020  xp=0.0015644909581  expected=0.0015644909899
    i=2, i=3 likewise (f32-level agreement on all four)
H = 1/sqrt(128) as expected. So exl3xhat (H_in on the input axis, su folded in) is RIGHT, and the
layout is W^T ([input][output]). The remaining error is therefore confined to:
  (a) exl3mv1s4's slab/decode or its down-column dots, or
  (b) exl3yhat (H_out on the output axis) / the sv application.
NEXT (one run, isolates (a) from (b)): run the fused path with xp REPLACED by a synthetic one-hot
input (xp = e_0) — exl3xhat skipped. Then u[n] = T[0][n] (the raw decoded tile row 0) and, with
sv folded, y = H_out(sv . T[0][:]). Compare those against the same quantity computed from the
VERIFIED buffer: T[0][n] can be recovered from B because B[k][n] = sum_c H[k][c] T[c][n] su[k] sv[n]
and H is orthogonal, so T = H^T (B / (su.sv)) row-wise — i.e. the fusion's own intermediate is
recoverable from the known-good W. That closes the loop without needing the python oracle.
=== ROUND 26: TRUE ONE-HOT — THE FUSED PATH IS DEFINITIVELY WRONG; WHERE IT IS NARROWED ===
With a CORRECT x = e_0 (the round-23 vector bug fixed: x[0] = 1.0, not the ramp):
    fused y[0] =  0.00588   B[0][0] = -0.01945
    fused y[1] =  0.00358   B[0][1] =  0.01580
    fused y[2] =  0.00353   B[0][2] = -0.02862
    ONESHOT_GATE max_abs 0.0938 over 2044 of 2048 elements.
VERIFIED AND TRUSTWORTHY: the layout (B = W^T, [input 4096][output 2048], stride 2048); the old
path (y = B row for a one-hot); exl3xhat (xp == su.H.x to f32 on four elements); H = 1/sqrt(128).
NOT VERIFIED AND WRONG: the end-to-end fused matvec. The defect is therefore inside exl3mv1s4's
slab/decode/down-column dots or exl3yhat's output transform — the two pieces with no independent
check yet.
HOW TO FINISH THIS (a focused kernel session, ~2-3 h, no new concept needed):
  1. dump CTA 0's decoded slab (8 input rows x 128 output cols) to global and compare against
     native/exl3/exl3_ref.py's T for the same tile — the decode is then either proven or the bug
     is obviously there (it is one element-mapping: slot/tile16/nibble walk copied from a kernel
     that works, so suspect the ROW mapping rb64*64+cta*8 vs the sample's own).
  2. with the decode proven, yhat is a 128x128 matvec whose input (u) can be dumped directly and
     compared against sv . T^T xp computed on the host from the dumped slab.
  3. then scale to the 8-expert FFN and re-measure.
STATUS OF THE PROGRAM: the fused path is NOT production-usable yet. The GREEN deliverables stand

=== ROUND 28: THE ALGEBRAIC BUG FOUND (sv ordering) — and a new symptom ===
Host-side reference (no kernel loads; T cast to f64 on the device, compared in Vyb host code):
    U_DIFF count_over_1e-4=0 sum_absdiff=0     (fused yraw == sv.(T^T xp) over 2048 outputs)
    Y_DIFF count_over_1e-4=0 sum_absdiff=0     (fused y == H_out(u))
i.e. the whole fused chain is SELF-consistent against a reference built from the proven decode.
But it disagreed with the VERIFIED B, and expanding B's definition shows why:
    y[n] = sv[n] . sum_j H[n][j] . u[j]        <-- sv scales AFTER the Hadamard
Both sv (a diagonal on the output axis) and H act on the SAME axis and do NOT commute. The
kernel applied sv to u BEFORE yhat (phase 2), so it was consistently computing H(sv.u) instead of
sv.(H u). FIX APPLIED: phase 2 accumulates the raw u; exl3yhat takes sv (+32) and scales after.
NEW SYMPTOM: with the fix, the one-shot run returns y = 0 exactly and yraw appears 0 too — a
different failure than before, so the fix is not fully wired (suspect: the yhat param block or the
staging that zeroes yraw, or the dump path interfering). This is the first thing to check next.

(layout, decode bit-identical, vector pass, dots, H transform). The remaining work is small but
mechanical.

=== ROUND 32: MINIMAL E2E DRIVER — two real fixes, and a razor-sharp remaining clue ===

=== ROUND 35: INITCHECK NAMES THE FAULT CLASS — exl3xhat READS UNINITIALISED GLOBAL MEMORY ===
compute-sanitizer --tool initcheck on the lean driver: 100 "Uninitialized __global__ memory read of
size 4 bytes" ALL at the same instruction (exl3xhat+0x1e0), across many threads of block 0. This
is the fault class that explains every symptom: uninitialised device memory is stale garbage whose
contents differ run to run, so the outputs it feeds are wrong in a way that varies between runs
(exactly the first_mismatch 128/224/352/384 pattern) while elements that happen not to touch it
(e.g. y[0]) stay correct to 1e-9. racecheck being clean is consistent: this is not a race, it is a
read-before-write.

report persists, so the uninitialised buffer is one of xhat's remaining inputs — the Hadamard
hd_pre32 (or memory just past a buffer it indexes). exl3hgen32 itself looks complete (grid 64x256
= 16384 threads, guard gid<16384, one f32 per thread), so the next step is to print the device
pointers and map the reported address (e.g. 0xfcb7c9018000) to the buffer it lands in.

stride (one Hadamard row) — PAST the 64 KB H. Cause: exl3xhat and exl3yhat index the 128x128
Hadamard with tid_x(), but launch_kernel ALWAYS launches 256 threads, so threads 128..255 read
H[d*128+j] up to d=255 (offset 0x1FFFC) and yhat wrote y[] past its buffer too. FIXED with

=== ROUND 64: THE GATHER IS 13x — AND THE BAR IS A BATCHING QUESTION, NOT A KERNEL ONE ===
Gather-cost spike (mvnod5 vs mvnod4, same load volume, 20 launches each, same expert/projection):
    real gather  0.65 ms   ->  6.5 GB/s effective
    no gather    0.05 ms   -> 84.0 GB/s effective      RATIO 13x
So the per-element slot addressing THE ENTIRE problem; the arithmetic, LUT and dot are nearly free.
Repacked format (precompute w16, 2 bytes/element = 4x footprint, ~702 GB for the model):
    0.30 ms/projection -> step 365 ms -> 2.74 tok/s (17x off the bar)
THE DECISIVE ARITHMETIC: the bar (21.1 ms/step) needs 5.1 GB of weight traffic per step in 21 ms =
242 GB/s, i.e. 89% of the GB10's 273 GB/s HBM peak. SINGLE-STREAM DECODE CANNOT REACH THAT ON THIS
HARDWARE — no kernel, no format and no node count changes it, because the weights alone do not fit in
the time budget at peak bandwidth.
What the 45-50 tok/s figure almost certainly IS: aggregate throughput with batching, where one weight
read serves many tokens. Our measured single-stream cost, amortised over a batch:
    batch  1 -> 850 ms   |  4 -> 212 ms  |  16 -> 53 ms  |  32 -> 27 ms  |  64 -> 13 ms
So at batch 32 the fused path is 27 ms/token-equivalent (37 tok/s) and at batch 64 it is 13 ms
(77 tok/s) — i.e. THE BAR IS A BATCH-SIZE QUESTION. Beating vLLM means running a batched decode
(weights amortised) with the fused kernel, not optimising the single-stream path further.
=== END (rounds 20-64) ===

    B=32  mv 0.60 ms   -> 0.01875     <- flat to within 20% over a 32x batch increase
Numerics unchanged: the single-token gate still reads 0/2048 over 1e-4, max 7.9e-07.
Projected from the measured per-token cost (45 layers x 9 experts x 3 projections):
    matvec-only step at B=32 : 22.8 ms/token -> 43.9 tok/s
    competitive bar          : 21.1 ms/token -> 45-50 tok/s   (matvec is 1.08x the whole bar budget)
    with the 10x gather win  : 2.3 ms -> 439 tok/s of matvec, leaving 19 ms for everything else
SO: THE BAR IS REACHABLE AND THE ROUTE IS BATCHING plus the gather repack, not single-stream latency.
Single-stream (B=1) is bandwidth-capped at ~1.2 tok/s and always will be; a batched server amortic
the 4.2 MB expert read across 32 tokens and lands in the same range as vLLM.
WHAT REMAINS TO MAKE THIS A SERVED NUMBER (not a projection): attention + router + the Hadamard
xhat/yhat passes per token, and a real batch scheduler. The matvec — which was the hard part — is done.
=== END (rounds 20-65) ===
=== ROUND 66: THE REMAINING WORK IS TWO MECHANICAL STEPS, AND TOGETHER THEY BEAT THE BAR ===
Measured per-token cost of one projection at B=32:
    matvec  0.01875 ms
    xhat    0.03125 ms      <- exactly ONE LAUNCH OVERHEAD: the Hadamard passes are still
    yhat    0.03125 ms         launched once per TOKEN, so at B=32 they are 77% of the work
    total   0.08125 ms/token
Projected step (45 layers x 9 experts x 3 projections):
    as measured (hadamard unbatched)        98.7 ms ->  10.1 tok/s
    hadamard batched (1 launch per B)       27.6 ms ->  36.2 tok/s
    hadamard batched + 10x gather repack     7.1 ms -> 140.1 tok/s
    competitive bar                         21.1 ms ->  45-50 tok/s
SO THE ROADMAP TO *BEAT* THE BAR IS TWO MECHANICAL CHANGES, NO NEW RESEARCH:
    1. batch the xhat/yhat launches (grid covers B tokens instead of looping tokens)  -> ~36 tok/s
    2. the gather repack measured in round 64 (13x on the matvec)                     -> ~140 tok/s
Both are ordinary kernel plumbing. The hard part (a correct, W-free, weight-amortising fused decode
matvec) is done and validated. Attention and the router still have to be added on top, which is why
this is a roadmap and not a served number yet.
=== END (rounds 20-66) ===
=== ROUND 67: HADAMARD PASSES BATCHED — STEP 30.3 ms = 33.0 tok/s, AND 102 tok/s WITH THE REPACK ===
xhatB / yhatB take the whole batch in ONE launch (grid = B * blocks-per-token). Measured, 10 launches
divided by B=32:
    xhat  0.03125 -> 0.0031 ms/token   (10x, exactly the launch overhead removed)
    yhat  0.03125 -> 0.0031 ms/token
    gate  still 0/2048 over 1e-4, max 8.6e-07
Per projection at B=32: 0.01875 (mv) + 0.0031 + 0.0031 = 0.02495 ms/token.
    step now                30.3 ms ->  33.0 tok/s
    bar                     21.1 ms ->  45-50 tok/s
    + 10x gather repack      9.8 ms -> 102   tok/s  (2.2x PAST the bar)
So after two rounds of ordinary plumbing the MoE path is within ~30% of the competitive bar, and the
measured gather repack takes it decisively past it. Remaining for a SERVED number: attention, the
router, and a real batch scheduler — none of which are measured yet.
=== END (rounds 20-67) ===
=== ROUND 68: CORRECTION — THE GATHER REPACK IS NOT THE 10x I CLAIMED ===
Round 64/66 said the 13x no-gather result meant a repack would take the step to 102 tok/s. That was
wrong, and here is why: the synthetic no-gather variant read 4 bytes per element from the SAME 128-byte
tile, so it had ~8x LESS unique traffic than a real repack would. The true arithmetic:
    tile = 16x16 = 256 elements sharing 128 bytes; each element needs 4 byte-reads
      -> 1024 reads of 128 unique bytes = 8x redundancy
    a real repack (4 contiguous bytes per element) = 33.6 MB per projection = 8x the packed size
    model-wide that is ~1.4 TB (lefty checkpoint 164 GB, righty free 385 GB) -> INFEASIBLE
    and the payoff is 0.01875 -> 0.005-0.0125 ms/token (1.5-3.7x), not 10x
So: NO REPACK. The 13x figure measured addressing cost under an unrealistically small working set;
it does not translate into a cheap offline transform. Round 67's 102 tok/s row is withdrawn.
THE HONEST NUMBER REMAINS 33.0 tok/s (30.3 ms/step) for the MoE path, and the remaining lever is
BATCH SIZE, not the kernel: the matvec stayed flat from B=1 to B=32 (0.5 -> 0.6 ms while the batch
grew 32x), so pushing B to 64/128 amortises the same 4.2 MB read over more tokens. That sweep is
running now.
=== END (rounds 20-68) ===
=== ROUND 69: BATCH 128 CLEARS THE BAR (52.8 tok/s projected from measured matvec) ===
Extended batch sweep, this run, same validated expert (gate still 0/2048, max 8.3e-07):
    B=  1 mv 0.60 ms -> 0.60000 ms/token
    B=  2 mv 0.60     -> 0.30000
    B=  4 mv 0.60     -> 0.15000
    B=  8 mv 0.60     -> 0.07500
    B= 16 mv 0.50     -> 0.03125
    B= 32 mv 0.70     -> 0.021875
    B= 64 mv 0.80     -> 0.012500
    B=128 mv 1.20     -> 0.009375
The matvec grows only 2x while the batch grows 128x: the expert's 4.2 MB is read once per batch.
With the measured batched-Hadamard cost (0.0031 ms/token each) the step projects to:
    B=  1 : 736.5 ms ->  1.4 tok/s
    B= 32 :  34.1 ms -> 29.3 tok/s
    B= 64 :  22.7 ms -> 44.0 tok/s     <- at the bar
    B=128 :  19.0 ms -> 52.8 tok/s     <- CLEARS the 45-50 tok/s bar by ~11%
NO repack needed (round 68 withdrew it). The lever is batch size, exactly as the bandwidth arithmetic
said it had to be. CAVEAT, stated plainly: this is the MoE path only — attention, the router and the
batch scheduler are still unmeasured, and they consume the same 19 ms budget. It is a projection from
measured kernels, not a served throughput number.

    self_attn.q_b_proj    BF16 [16384,1536]    50.3 MB
    self_attn.kv_b_proj   BF16 [32768,512]     33.6 MB
    self_attn.q_a_proj    BF16 [1536,4096]     12.6 MB
    kv_a_proj_with_mqa, the DeepSeek-style indexer, norms ... ~19 MB
    ---------------------------------------------------------------
    layer-3 attention total  249.8 MB  -> x45 = 11.2 GB PER STEP (dense, not amortisable by batch)
    MoE packed                5.1 GB per step

=== ROUND 71: BF16 STREAMING MEASURED AT 223.7 GB/s — THE STEP IS CLOSED AT B=128 ===

    223.7 GB/s (82% of the 273 GB/s peak — a realistic, unobstructed number for this device)
    => attention's 11.2 GB per step costs 50.1 ms/step
FULL STEP AT BATCH 128, every term measured:
    MoE (fused, batched)      2,430 ms   (1215 projections x 2.0 ms, measured kernels)

So the model as it exists (EXL3 MoE + BF16 MLA attention) runs above the competitive bar at batch 128,

=== END (rounds 20-71) ===
=== ROUND 72: MEASURED SERVE LOOP — 1,463 ms PER STEP AT B=128 = 87.5 tok/s ===
The projection in round 71 assumed 1215 launches x 2.0 ms = 2.43 s. The REAL loop (45 layers x
9 experts x 3 projections = 1215 fused matvec launches at B=128, timed end to end with the host loop
and launch overhead included) measured:
    P6R10 SERVE_LOOP launches=1215 batch=128 step_ms=1463 tok_per_s=87.49
    gate still exact: 0/2048 over 1e-4, max 8.9e-07
It is FASTER than the projection because the launches queue asynchronously and the GPU pipelines
them — the previous figure summed per-launch wall times as if they were serial.
Adding the measured attention term (50 ms of BF16 streaming for 11.2 GB):
    ~1,513 ms per step -> 84.6 tok/s AGGREGATE at batch 128
    competitive bar       45-50 tok/s
CAVEAT, stated plainly: the loop reuses ONE expert's weights 1215 times, so that 4.2 MB is L2-resident
and this does not pay the 5.1 GB of real per-step weight streaming. That is acceptable here because
the fused kernel runs at ~6.5 GB/s effective — far below the 223 GB/s the device sustains — so it is
gather-bound, not bandwidth-bound, and caching the weights does not change the measured compute. A
real serve loop must still confirm this with distinct experts (different shards, cold cache).
=== END (rounds 20-72) ===
=== ROUND 73: COLD-CACHE SERVE LOOP CONFIRMED — 91.2 tok/s AT B=128 ===
Rotating the 1215 launches through 21 DIFFERENT layers' experts (88 MB of packed weights, well past
any L2), resolved with each layer's own tab (the cache is 0-indexed by layer — two off-by-one bugs in
my own driver had to be fixed first: one silently collapsed the rotation to a single layer, the other
resolved nothing at all):
    P6R10 SERVE_SETS n=21 packed_MB=88
    P6R10 SERVE_LOOP_COLD launches=1215 batch=128 sets=21 step_ms=1404 tok_per_s=91.17
    gate still exact: 0/2048 over 1e-4, max 8.6e-07
The cold result (91.2 tok/s) matches the warm ones (87.5 / 95.0) within run-to-run noise, which
CONFIRMS what the bandwidth arithmetic predicted: the fused kernel is gather-bound (~6.5 GB/s
effective) and not bandwidth-bound (223.7 GB/s available), so weight locality does not matter at this
scale and the 5.1 GB/step of real streaming is not the limiter.
FINAL ANSWER TO THE COMPETITIVE BAR, with every term measured on this device:
    MoE batched serve loop (1,215 launches, B=128, 88 MB cold weights)  1,404 ms
    attention (11.2 GB BF16 at the measured 223.7 GB/s)                    50 ms
    ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    ~1,454 ms per step for 128 tokens -> 88.0 tok/s AGGREGATE
    competitive bar (vLLM)                        45-50 tok/s
=> ~1.8x ABOVE the bar, single GB10 node, EXL3-4bpw model as shipped, no repack, no distillation.
STILL NOT COUNTED: the router (2.4 MB BF16/layer), the indexer/rope/softmax work, and the scheduler.
Those are ordinary per-layer pieces; they will cost something, but the margin is ~1.8x.
=== END (rounds 20-73) ===
=== ROUND 74: WHAT A REAL BATCHED SERVE PATH ACTUALLY NEEDS (scope, not a start) ===
Asked to turn the 88 tok/s projection into a served endpoint. Surveyed the engine first, and the
honest answer is that this is a multi-day build, not a next step. The gap, concretely:
 1. NO BATCHED BF16 GEMM PRIMITIVE. Attention is BF16 (o_proj 134 MB, q_b 50 MB, kv_b 34 MB ...) and
    the engine multiplies it with gemm32 (f32, SIMT). A batched B-token attention path needs a bf16
    GEMM (or a tensor-core one, which Vyb has no intrinsics for yet). Nothing exists to reuse.
 2. ATTENTION IS SINGLE-TOKEN in glm_serve.vyb: the MLA structure (q_a -> norm -> q_b, kv_a ->
    kv_b, the DeepSeek-style indexer) is written per token, with rope/softmax interleaved. Batching it
    means restructuring the hot loop of an ~8,000-line file AND adding a KV cache keyed by batch slot.
 3. NO SCHEDULER. glm_serve.vyb takes one request at a time; continuous batching needs a queue, a
    batch builder, per-slot KV bookkeeping and a token streamer.
EFFORT, honestly: 1 is ~a day (kernel + validation vs gemm32), 2 is the big one (~2-3 days plus a
re-validation of the whole 45-layer chain against the existing digests), 3 is ~a day on top of 2.
WHAT DOES NOT NEED REDOING: the MoE path. The fused batched matvec, the batched Hadamard passes and
the measured 1,404 ms/step serve loop are done, exact, and cold-cache verified.
RECOMMENDATION: do 1 (the bf16 GEMM) as the next self-contained step — it is small, it is the missing
primitive, and it can be validated in isolation the same way mvnod4 was. Then attack 2 with the
45-layer digest gate as the safety net.
=== END (rounds 20-74) ===
=== ROUND 75: bfgemm_p WRITTEN AND COMPILED — NOT YET VALIDATED (stated plainly) ===
The missing primitive for batched attention: native/kernels/bfgemm.vyb, a faithful port of the proven
split-K gemm32_p that reads BF16 weights directly with the language's ld_bf16 (no dequant pass, no f32
copy of the weights), keeping the 4-output-rows-per-thread structure and the Float32-bound MACs that
the original needed to stay at mul.rn.f32 rate. It also gained a TRANSP flag for the checkpoint's
[out][in] layout (correct but uncoalesced; production should transpose once at load).
    ptxas: "PTX contains all 1 device function(s)" / "validated (sm_86)" -- it COMPILES.
IT IS NOT VALIDATED. I started a validation driver and hit a real problem: building BF16 weights on
the host side needs an encoder Vyb does not expose (bf16 rounding is the GPU's ld_bf16), so a synthetic
case cannot be constructed from the host, and a real-data case needs a slice of the safetensors file
with its header offsets. I deleted the half-written driver rather than ship a kernel with an
unvalidated claim attached.
VALIDATION PLAN for the next session (one of):
  a) slice the real o_proj BF16 file (say N=64, K=256) and compare against a host dot product -- 2M
     MACs, fast, and it exercises the real addressing and conversion;
  b) feed the same weights through bf16deq32 -> f32 and run gemm32_p/r as the reference, comparing
     the two kernels on identical values (needs both to use the TRANSP layout).
Until one of those passes, DO NOT use bfgemm_p in any path.
=== END (rounds 20-75) ===
=== ROUND 76: bfgemm VALIDATION BLOCKED BY A SILENT-ZEROS TRAP (worth knowing) ===
Wrote native/host/p6r12_bfgemm.vyb: reads layers.3.self_attn.indexer.wk.weight [128,4096] BF16 (1 MB),
converts it to f32 on device with the existing bf16deq32, computes a host reference for M=2 x N=128 over
the full K=4096, then runs bfgemm_p (TRANSP=1, KSP=1) and compares.
RESULT: BFG_GATE count_over_1e-4=0 max_abs=0 -- but the tensor came back ALL ZEROS (w[0][0]=0), so the
comparison was 0 vs 0: an INVALID validation, not a passing one.
ROOT CAUSE FOUND: the on-disk tensor is fine. A direct byte read of shard model-00005-of-00083 shows
524,287 of 524,288 u16 words non-zero, first words [15680,-16976,15808,...]. So gx_read returned zeros
because MY RESOLVE FAILED and I never checked `found`. That is a silent-zeros trap in the probe style:
gx_resolve_tab / gx_read return a correctly-SIZED, all-zero buffer when the name is not in the tab,
which looks exactly like a real tensor of zeros. ANY future probe MUST assert found == 1 before using
the data, and should sanity-check one element against the raw file.
NEXT STEP (small): assert found, and if the tab lacks the attention entry, add it (the 45-layer chain
does resolve self_attn.q_b_proj.weight, so the tab format supports it -- it is likely my layer-3 table
entry list that is short). Then the bfgemm validation runs for real.
=== END (rounds 20-76) ===
=== ROUND 77: gx_read RETURNS ZEROS FOR ATTENTION TENSORS IN THIS PROBE (found == 1) ===
Hardened the resolve (candidate list + a real `found` assertion), and it does find the tensor:
    BFG try ...self_attn.kv_a_proj_with_mqa.weight found=1 bytes=4194304
    BFG using N=512 K=4096
    BFG converted 2097152 values; w[0][0]=0 w[1][1]=0      <- STILL ALL ZEROS
And the on-disk data is definitely non-zero: a direct safetensors read gives
    kv_a_proj_with_mqa.weight  shard 00005, 4194304 bytes, first words [-17360, 15458, -17413, -17617]
    q_a_proj.weight            shard 00005, 12582912 bytes, first words [-17376, 15381, -17845, 15520]
So: found=1, correct byte count, correct shape, and the bytes on disk are real -> but gx_read hands back a
zero buffer. The 45-layer chain reads these same attention tensors correctly (the model produces the
right digests), so this is either (a) my probe's usage of gx_read/gx_resolve_tab, or (b) something about
the tab I am using for layer 3 in a probe context. It is NOT a broken checkpoint and NOT a broken kernel.
STATUS OF bfgemm_p: still NOT validated. It compiles and ptxas accepts it; its correctness is unknown.
ONE FOCUSED DEBUG needed (next session): compare against how glm_serve.vyb reads a self_attn tensor --
same resolve call, same gx_read, print the first 4 bytes straight after the read -- rather than guessing.
=== END (rounds 20-77) ===
=== ROUND 78: bfgemm_p VALIDATED (addressing + accumulation) — two bugs found on the way ===
BUG 1 (the silent-zeros cause): my probe called gx_read TWICE on the same GxLoc (once for .len(),
once for the buffer). The second read returns an all-zero buffer of the right size. Reading ONCE and
reusing the buffer fixes it -- first bytes are now 48,188,98,60 = 0x30BC, exactly the -17360 word that
a direct safetensors read of shard 00005 shows. RULE: one gx_read per GxLoc per probe.
BUG 2 (values): my hand-rolled bf16 conversion path (htod + bf16deq32 with (q,o,n,z)) produced zeros;
the engine's deq_bf16(fbf, rawString, n, z) works. Use the engine helper.
RESULT: with real tensor bytes, bfgemm_p vs a host dot product over the SAME decoded values:
    BFG_GATE count_over_1e-4 = 0   max_abs = 2.37e-11   (M=2 x N=64 over K=4096)
Since the reference uses deq_bf16 output while the kernel reads BF16 with ld_bf16, agreement at 2e-11
means the kernel's BF16 read path, its [N][K] TRANSP addressing, its 4-rows-per-thread structure and
its split-K accumulation all match the production decoder. bfgemm_p is VALIDATED for addressing and
accumulation and can be used in the batched attention path.
CAVEAT: the decoded weights in this test came out denormal (w ~ 1e-18), so the test's DYNAMIC RANGE is
weak -- it proves the two paths agree, not that they agree well at O(1) magnitudes. A follow-up with
real-magnitude weights would strengthen it. Note also that o[0]=0 because the dot of near-zero values
is near zero.
=== END (rounds 20-78) ===
=== ROUND 79: CORRECTION — THE DOUBLE gx_read WAS NEVER PROVEN; THE CONVERSION WAS THE BUG ===
Round 78 blamed the silent zeros on calling gx_read twice per GxLoc. The two runs side by side show
that is not established:
    single read + manual (htod + bf16deq32)  : first4=48,188,98,60 (CORRECT), w[0][0]=0        <- zeros
    single read + engine deq_bf16            : first4=48,188,98,60 (CORRECT), w[0][0]=7.8e-18   <- data
The bytes were right in BOTH cases, so the confirmed cause of the zeros is the HAND-ROLLED CONVERSION
path, not the read. The double-read runs (rounds 76-77) never printed first4, so whether the second
read also returns zeros is UNPROVEN. I should not have written it as a rule.
WHAT IS ACTUALLY ESTABLISHED:
  - use the engine's deq_bf16(fbf, rawString, n, z); a manual htod + bf16deq32 with (q,o,n,z) gave zeros
  - reading once and reusing the buffer is still the sane pattern, just not a proven fix
  - bfgemm_p agrees with deq_bf16 to 2.4e-11 (round 78), on weights that decoded to ~1e-18 magnitudes
=== ROUND 80: THE ATTENTION BUILD IS BLOCKED AT STEP 0 — NO VERIFIED BF16 WEIGHT LOAD ===
Starting the batched MLA attention build exposed a blocker that must be fixed before any of it is
worth writing, and it is worth recording precisely because it invalidates part of round 78's result.
FACTS:
  - gx_read gives the right BYTES for a real attention tensor. layers.3.self_attn.kv_a_proj_with_mqa
    .weight -> first bytes 48,188 = 0xBC30 little-endian, which is exactly the -17360 word a direct
    safetensors read of shard 00005 shows. The String is correct.
  - little-endian word 0xBC30 as bf16 is -0.0107422 (sign 1, exp 0x78, mantissa 0x30).
  - My manual path (cuMemcpyHtoD_v2 + bf16deq32 with q,o,n,z) produced w[0][0] = 0        <- zeros
  - The engine helper deq_bf16(fbf, g0, n, 0) produced w[0][0] = 7.806e-18, whose top 16 bits are
    0x2310 -- i.e. it decoded a DIFFERENT word than the String holds, so its upload path differs.
CONSEQUENCE FOR ROUND 78: bfgemm_p "agreeing with deq_bf16 to 2.4e-11" only shows the two GPU bf16
decoders agree with each other on whatever buffer they were given. Since the buffer's contents are
now in doubt, that validation is weaker than stated: it validates bfgemm_p's addressing and split-K
accumulation, NOT that it reads real checkpoint weights correctly.
STEP 0 (must do first, small): upload a known BF16 tensor and prove ONE value round-trips --
0xBC30 -> -0.0107422 -- through the same upload path the EXL3 trellis uses (which is bit-exact).
Only then build q_a / kv_a / q_b / kv_b / o_proj on bfgemm_p.
=== END (rounds 20-80) ===
=== ROUND 81: STEP 0 FAILS — A KNOWN BF16 WORD DOES NOT SURVIVE THE UPLOAD ===
Minimal possible test (native/kernels/tobg.vyb: nothing but ld_bf16 + st_f32, params via ld_i64;
native/host/p6r13_step0.vyb: ONE gx_read, upload, convert 8 elements):
    S0 found=1 bytes=4194304
    S0 host bytes: 48,188,98,60          <- 0xBC30 little-endian, provably the on-disk word
    S0 device values: 7.806e-18 -1.282e-23 7.858e-24 -5.170e-26
    S0_FAIL expected -0.0107421875
Uploading straight from the String (rather than a substring) changes nothing, so substring was not it.
EVERY value is denormal, i.e. the device is reading words whose exponent field is ~0 while the host
holds real bytes. So the String->device transfer is putting different bytes on the device than the
String contains -- and this is the SAME operation the EXL3 trellis path performs successfully
(cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(arA, g0, g0.len()) gives bit-exact decode results), which means the difference lies
in something I have not identified about how that path uploads.
CONSEQUENCE: the batched MLA attention build CANNOT START until one known value round-trips. This is
now the single blocking item for that workstream, and it is a TOOLING question, not a model or kernel
question. Next session should read gx_read's implementation and the trellis upload call directly
(does gx_read return raw bytes, or does the trellis path use a different transfer helper?) rather than
continuing to infer from behaviour.
=== END (rounds 20-81) — STOPPING HERE ===
=== ROUND 82: STEP 0 — htod RETURNS 0 WHILE NOTHING LANDS; THE SOURCE STRING IS THE SUSPECT ===
Two more facts, and they narrow it to the String representation:
    godzilla: found=1 bytes=0 (the tensor is not on that box) -> host bytes 0,0,0,0,
              htod rc=0, device = garbage -> the copy reports SUCCESS while copying nothing
    lefty   : bytes=4194304, host bytes 48,188 48,188,98,60 (correct),
              device values all denormal (~1e-18..1e-26)
rc=0 with garbage downstream means the failure is not an error being swallowed -- it is what the
source pointer looks like to the binding. The denormal magnitudes are the signature of reading the
wrong 16-bit halves, i.e. Vyb's String does not lay its characters out the way a raw byte copy
assumes (UTF-8/wide encoding), while gx_read + the trellis path clearly get raw bytes somehow.
STOPPING HERE. The next action is to READ, not to infer:
    1. how gx_read stores its result (raw bytes, or code points in a wide buffer?)
    2. what cuMemcpyHtoD_v2 does with a String argument (does it take .data()? an encoded form? a
       length prefix?), and why the trellis path's call of the same shape works
    3. whether there is a dedicated raw-bytes upload helper that the trellis path actually uses
I have now spent several rounds inferring this from behaviour; the answer is in two function bodies.
=== END (rounds 20-82) — STOPPING ===
=== ROUND 83: THE THREE QUESTIONS ANSWERED — AND THE UPLOAD STILL FAILS VIA EVERY ROUTE ===
(1) gx_read(g,i): opens g.paths[i], read_at(f, g.off[i], g.nbytes[i]) -> a Vyb String of raw bytes.
    It also returns "" when found != 1 (which is the silent-zeros trap I hit earlier).
(2) cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(dstDevice<Int>, srcHost<loc<CVoid>>, n) takes a RAW POINTER, not a String. The
    binding's own comment explains the trap: "ALL raw-pointer work ... stays inside these freedom
    blocks, where the FFI pointer intrinsics are in scope (enabled only in modules that declare FFI
    pointer functions -- an importer does NOT inherit them, so no pointer types leak out)".
    At top level a String is therefore passed as its STRUCT (a host pointer), which is exactly what
    the device showed: "raw words on device" = 2.22e31, 1.41e35 ... i.e. pointer bits, not 48176.
(3) YES, the dedicated helper exists: cuda_memcpy_h2d(dst<Int>, src<String>, n<Int>) in
    bindings/cuda/mod.vyb, which checks n <= src.len() and does the copy inside a freedom block. It
    is imported as  import mod::{cuda_memcpy_h2d}  (the module file is mod.vyb).
AND IT STILL FAILS: calling cuda_memcpy_h2d gives rc=0 and the SAME pointer-bit garbage on the device,
and a freedom block written by hand in the driver does the same. Meanwhile the EXL3 trellis path
uploads multi-MB Strings and decodes them bit-exactly, so SOME route works -- but not any of the three
I can construct from the driver.
STOPPING. This needs hands-on debugging by someone who can put a breakpoint on the copy (or who knows
whether `freedom` + String->loc conversion in an *importer* module is silently degraded -- the comment
implies it is, yet cuda_memcpy_h2d lives in the right module and still does not land the bytes, which
suggests the failure is upstream of the copy, e.g. the String gx_read returns is not a plain byte
buffer for a 4 MB read).
=== END (rounds 20-83) — STOPPING, NOT BLOCKED ON MY OWN REASONING ANY FURTHER ===
=== ROUND 84: THE WORKING PATTERN FOUND — AND IT DOES NOT REPRODUCE IN A DRIVER main() ===
The working upload sites all do this, with the String passed DIRECTLY (no cast, no helper):
    native/glm53/glm_engine.vyb:299   hrc  = cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(q,   raw, raw.len())
    native/glm53/glm_engine.vyb:328   hrcf = cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(qf,  raw, raw.len())
    native/glm53/glm_engine.vyb:575   rc   = cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(dst, raw, raw.len())
    native/glm53/exl3_load.vyb:66     cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(qd, trellis, tb)
and each of those FILES declares the extern itself:
    extern "C" { cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(dstDevice<Int>, srcHost<loc<CVoid>>, ByteCount<Int>)<CInt> }
So the rule is: the CALLING module must declare the extern; that is what puts the String->loc<CVoid>
conversion in scope. I copied that exact declaration into my driver and called it inside a freedom
block -- and the device STILL holds pointer-bit junk (identical 2.2223504967623686e+31 etc. on lefty,
a different junk value on godzilla where bytes=0).
WHAT IS NOW ESTABLISHED PRECISELY, for Rick:
  - the same source line works inside a MODULE (glm_engine/exl3_load) and fails inside a host DRIVER
    with a main() -- the extern is declared in both cases, freedom is used in both cases
  - the copied bytes never arrive (a raw u16 readback shows 0x738C4002-class garbage, not 0xBC30)
  - rc is 0 in every variant, so nothing reports failure
  - wr_pdev's pattern -- cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(p + off, loc(v), 8) with a LOCAL SCALAR -- does work from
    the same driver, so the driver CAN upload; it is specifically the String argument form that fails
NEXT BEST MOVES (for someone with the compiler): (a) check whether String->loc<CVoid> conversion is
implemented only for module-scope calls and silently degrades in a script main(); (b) if so, use
loc(v) over a byte Vec the way wr_pdev does, or move the upload into glm_engine (deq_bf16 already
lives there -- but note deq_bf16 ALSO returned a wrong word (0x2310 instead of 0xBC30), so it needs
the same scrutiny).
=== END (rounds 20-84) ===
=== ROUND 85: MY PROBE IS NOT TRUSTWORTHY — NO COMPILER BUG IS PROVEN, AND I DID NOT FIX ONE ===
I was asked to fix the compiler. I did NOT make any compiler change, because the evidence turned and
the honest conclusion is that my own probe is the more likely defect.
THE TURN: I added two controls.
  1. write KNOWN words with wr_pdev (a path proven across this codebase) then decode:
         wr_pdev(dS, 0, 48176); wr_pdev(dS, 8, 15458)
         -> "KNOWN first=1.56e-314 second=0"  and  "KNOWN words=5.90e-315 0"   (expect -0.0107 / 48176)
  2. a kernel that stores the constant 42.0 and reads it back:
         -> "const values = 51539616020 51539616020"                          (expect 42 42)
A kernel that does nothing but store a constant cannot obtain 5.15e10, and host-written 48176 cannot
read back as 0. So the fault is in my probe's plumbing (param buffer reuse, f32/f64 width in the
readback, or the store address), NOT necessarily in the String upload and NOT demonstrated to be in
the compiler.
CONSEQUENCE — the earlier conclusion must be withdrawn: "the String->device upload places different
bytes" was inferred from readings produced by this same unreliable probe. It may still be true, but it
is NOT established, and I should not have escalated it to a compiler bug.
STATE: p6r13_step0.vyb now carries both controls; they fail. Until a kernel that stores a constant and
reads it back gets 42, no measurement from this probe means anything.
WHAT I RECOMMEND: not a compiler change. First make the probe demonstrably sane (constant round-trip),
then re-ask whether the String upload is wrong at all. The compiler's String->loc<CVoid> conversion is
exonerated for now: real modules (glm_engine:299/328/575, exl3_load:66) upload multi-MB Strings and
their results are bit-exact, which is strong evidence the conversion works.
=== END (rounds 20-85) ===
=== ROUND 86: *** STEP 0 PASSES *** — THE COMPILER WAS NEVER BROKEN; IT WAS bulk_dtoh's WIDTH ===
  S0 DECODED first=-0.0107421875 second=0.0137939453125
  S0_PASS bf16 round-trip verified: 0xBC30 -> -0.0107421875
and the Python oracle on the same tensor agrees exactly: first 4 as bf16 = -0.010742188 0.013793945
-0.0076599121 -0.0026702881 (shard model-00005-of-00083, data_off 67874992, size 4194304, dtype BF16).
THE ROOT CAUSE, after ~15 rounds of chasing it (all of them my own probe's):
  1. bulk_dtoh(base, n) copies n*8 bytes and reads them as f64 (tensor.vyb:159). My probe's kernels
     stored f32, so EVERY readback was a width mismatch -- that is where 2.22e31, 51539616020 and
     7.8e-18 came from. None of it was ever evidence about uploads.
  2. bf16.vyb has TWO twins: bf16deq stores f64, bf16deq32 stores f32. I used deq32 and read with
     bulk_dtoh (f64) -> garbage. bf16deq with z=0 stores LINEAR f64 -> correct.
  3. z>0 means TRANSPOSED (rr=gid/z, cc=gid%z, at cc*(n/z)+rr) -- so passing z=K with a wrong n
     scrambles the layout. For a linear first-8 check, z must be 0.
  4. The String upload is FINE -- deq_bf16(faddr, rawString, n, z) passes the String straight to
     cuMemcpyHtoD_v2 (glm_engine:299) and lands the exact bytes. No hand-rolled copy is needed, and
     my hand-rolled addr()/at() attempt crashed the process (that at() pattern is for a Vec, not a String).
WITHDRAWN: the "String->device upload is broken" claim and the compiler-bug escalation. The compiler's
String handling is correct; the bug was a f32/f64 width mismatch in my own readback. Two rounds were
spent inferring a compiler defect from a mis-typed readback -- the lesson is to validate the probe
against a KNOWN value (this test does: 0xBC30 -> -0.0107421875, matching an independent Python oracle)
BEFORE drawing any conclusion from it.
NEXT: the attention build can now proceed -- bfgemm_p is validated and BF16 weights load correctly.
=== ROUND 87: bfgemm_p IS **NOT** VALIDATED — the 2.4e-11 WAS GARBAGE-vs-GARBAGE ===

    w[0][0]=-0.0107421875   w[1][1]=0.016845703125        <- weights now provably correct
    BFG_GATE count_over_1e-4=128  max_abs=0.77522620372474194  of 128   <- GEMM MISMATCHES
Round 78's "agrees with deq_bf16 to 2.37e-11" compared two near-zero denormal buffers: the reference

O(1e-2) weights the kernel is wrong on all 128 outputs, max error 0.7752.
SO: bfgemm_p's addressing / TRANSP / split-K needs debugging before ANY attention projection is built
on it. Do not trust the earlier PASS. Likely suspects (in order): the TRANSP=1 [N][K] indexing, the

which would produce exactly this "plausible but wrong" profile).
Lesson (twice in a row now): a numerical gate only means something when the INPUT magnitudes are
real. Both of today's withdrawn claims (String upload, bfgemm PASS) came from near-zero buffers.

  - SK: the kernel stores f32 partials but bulk_dtoh reads f64 -> added a widen32 kernel (f32->f64),

  BFG_GATE count_over_1e-4=128  max_abs=0.77522620372474194  of 128     and o[0]=0
with max_abs BIT-IDENTICAL to the run before any of the above fixes. Identical output from changed

KERNEL-SIDE NOTE: bfgemm_p's structure looks correct on inspection (pa0 = A + (mm0*K+k0)*4 stepping

=== ROUND 89: THE HAND-CHECKABLE CASE — bfgemm_p IS WRONG, WITH A SHARP SIGNATURE ===
native/host/p6r14_tiny.vyb: A = [1..8] f32, B = [N][K] = 2 rows of bf16 1.0 and 2.0,
M=1 N=2 K=8 KSP=1 TRANSP=1. Arithmetic by hand: o[0] = sum(1..8) = 36, o[1] = 2*36 = 72.
Both machines, identical and deterministic:
    T handles: gemm=... widen=... cast=...      (all resolve; grid=1 rc=0)
    T o[0]=-17.9375  o[1]=36                    (expect 36 and 72)
    T_FAIL d0=53.9375 d1=36
SIGNATURE: the arithmetically CORRECT row-0 dot (36) is sitting at index 1, index 0 holds garbage
(-17.9375), and row 1's correct 72 appears nowhere. So the accumulation is right and the EPILOGUE's
index mapping is off -- roughly "value for nn lands at nn+1, index 0 never written".
Where to look (all in bfgemm_p's epilogue): the store index (ks*total + mm0*N + nn)*4 versus the
thread mapping gid = nb*dim_x() + tid_x(); and whether the 4-row epilogue's guarded stores
(if (mm0+1 < M) ...) interact with M=1. On paper the index math reads correctly
(nn = gid % N; store at nn*4; store data at B + (nn*K + k0)*2), so this is likely a CODEGEN issue in
the 4-output-row epilogue rather than a source-arithmetic one -- which would also explain why the
real-tensor run was wrong in a "plausible but shifted" way rather than obviously broken.
NEXT: bisect the epilogue -- replace the 4-row store block with a single st_f32 for a0 only and re-run
this same tiny case. If [36, 72] appears, the guarded 4-row store is the defect.
=== ROUND 90: bfgemm_p BISECT — NOT the epilogue; a CONSTANT -17.9375 THAT IGNORES THE INPUTS ===
Three deterministic runs of the hand-checkable case (A=[1..8], B rows of bf16 1.0 / 2.0):
  N=2, guarded 4-row epilogue   (bfgemm_p) : slots [-17.9375, 36, 0,0,0,0]   expect [36, 72]
  N=2, single-store epilogue    (bfgemm_1s): slots [-17.9375, 36, 0,0,0,0]   identical
  N=1, single-store epilogue               : slots [-17.9375, 0, 0,0,0,0]    expect [36]
FACTS:
  - the epilogue is NOT the defect: at M=1 the guarded a1..a3 stores never fire, so both variants
    execute the same store, and both give identical output (my bisect was inconclusive by design).
  - slots 2..7 stay 0 in every run, and I zeroed dS first, so the printout shows EXACTLY two writes
    (N=2) / one write (N=1). The kernel does run, and it writes where it should.
  - -17.9375 is CONSTANT across N=1 and N=2, and it is not any function of the inputs I can construct:
    the dot of [1..8] with eight 1.0s is 36, with 2.0s is 72. Nothing yields -17.9375.
  - N=2 also writes 36 into slot 1 -- the CORRECT row-0 dot, in the WRONG slot; row 1's 72 never
    appears at all.
READING: the accumulation is not reading the buffers I think it is. Either A (the cast f64->f32) or B
is not landing, and the "36" is a coincidence of the same wrong read. Next probe should dump the
DEVICE's A and B as f64 right before the launch (widen32 + bulk_dtoh) and print them -- if A is not
exactly 1..8 or B is not 1.0/2.0, the GEMM was never given the inputs, and bfgemm_p may be innocent.
That check costs one run and eliminates the whole ambiguity.
=== ROUND 91: *** bfgemm_p PASSES THE HAND-CHECKABLE CASE *** (the bug was my bf16 packing) ===
  T A on device: 1 2 3 ... 8
  T B on device: 1 1 | 2 2
  T all8: 36 72 0 0 0 0
  T o[0]=36 o[1]=72   (expect 36 and 72)
  T_PASS bfgemm_p is correct on the hand-checkable case
THE BUG WAS MINE: I packed four bf16 words per 8-byte Int as a SUM OF SHIFTS
(0x3F80 + 0x3F8000 + 0x3F800000 + 0x3F80000000) which double-counted a term and produced
0xBF80 (=-1.0) in word 0. The device showed it immediately: "T B on device: -1 0 | 2 0".
Correct constants: w_one = 0x3F803F803F803F80, w_two = 0x4000400040004000.
So bfgemm_p's arithmetic, TRANSP addressing and KSP=1 epilogue are all CORRECT on a case whose answer
I can compute by hand. The earlier real-tensor failure (max_abs 0.7752, o[0]=0) therefore has a
DIFFERENT cause -- and the one remaining difference between the passing micro-test and the failing
real-tensor probe is how B reaches the device:
    micro-test : wr_pdev(dB, off, int)  -- 8-byte scalar writes          -> PASSES
    real probe : cuMemcpyHtoD_v2(dB, g0, N*K*2) with g0 a String        -> FAILS
That is the exact hypothesis to test next, and it can now be tested cleanly: upload the same real
tensor BOTH ways and compare a b0d decode of the first 8 words. If the String path disagrees, the
problem is the String->device copy in a driver; if it agrees, the problem is elsewhere in the probe.
STANDING MICRO-TEST: native/host/p6r14_tiny.vyb -- seconds to run, hand-checkable, covers M/N/KSP/
TRANSP/epilogue. Any future bfgemm change runs through it first.
